● Good corrections. Let me clarify each.

  cpp is the C preprocessor, not C++

  Sloppy naming on my part. I meant the C preprocessor — the standalone tool that handles #include, #define, macro expansion. It's a C
  utility, ships with gcc, and we're already shelling out to gcc for the compile step. To remove the ambiguity entirely we just invoke gcc -E
   (preprocess-only mode) which is what compilers actually call. No C++ in the pool runner.

  Library types vs spec rules — yes, separate

  Important distinction you drew, and it changes the design. Two different scopes:

  Library functions are immutable. Some 1990s C library function takes a void (*callback)(int) parameter and returns a struct rusage*. We
  don't get to refuse it. The parser walks the signature, identifies the un-serializable pieces, and the resulting "signature" carries flags
  like "param 2 is a callback hole" and "return value is a pointer to an opaque struct."

  The spec config is where the user resolves the holes. When the editor renders the box for this library function, the un-serializable
  parameters become editor slots that demand a resolution rule. Concretely, for each hole the user picks one of:

  - Wire from another box (the default for serializable params)
  - Pass a constant (the user types a literal value into a slot)
  - Pass NULL / a default zero (for optional params)
  - Callback into the graph (the parameter is a function pointer; the user picks a Lua/C/bash box that should run when the C library invokes
  the callback; SoraMech generates a C trampoline)
  - Ignore the return value (for ptr-to-opaque returns the user doesn't care about)

  The schema captures these choices per parameter and the wrapper generator emits the appropriate glue. Box JSON for a library box becomes
  something like:

  { "id": "qsort_box", "kind": "call", "lang": "c",
    "ref": "<stdlib.h>", "fn": "qsort", "link_libs": [],
    "param_rules": {
      "base":    "wire_in:items",
      "nmemb":   "wire_in:count",
      "size":    "constant:4",
      "compar":  "callback:Lua:compare_box"
    } }

  (Editor writes this; user doesn't type it.)

  This isolates the rule "errors loudly on un-serializable types" to only the user-written code case (where the user does control the API and
   can fix it). For library functions the parser produces a signature with holes, and the holes get filled in the spec config.

  "C is just built-ins and structs, right?"

  Almost. The full set of type forms in C is:

  - Primitives — int / long / char / double / etc, plus the signed/unsigned and width variants
  - Pointers — T* for any T
  - Arrays — T[N] (or T[] decayed at function-call sites)
  - Structs — collection of named fields
  - Unions — alternative interpretations of the same memory
  - Enums — named integer constants, but always backed by an int
  - Function pointers — a special-case pointer
  - typedefs — aliases for any of the above

  For JSON round-tripping:

  - Primitives → JSON number/boolean/string. Always works.
  - Pointer-to-primitive + length → "bytes" or "array of T." Works.
  - Pointer-to-struct + length → JSON array of objects. Works.
  - Structs → JSON objects. Works.
  - Arrays → JSON arrays. Works.
  - Enums → JSON number (or JSON string by name, our choice). Works.
  - Unions → ambiguous without a discriminator; user resolves in the spec config (which variant is active).
  - Function pointers → callback holes per above.
  - Pointers without a length → ambiguous (how many elements?); user resolves in the spec config (either "one element" / "null-terminated" /
  "size from param N").

  So the parser walks any C type tree down to primitives and produces a serialization plan. The plan can have unresolved nodes (callback
  holes, union discriminator unknowns, length-unknown pointers) that the spec config fills in.

  C struct → Lua table round-trip

  Yes, this works cleanly. C → JSON → Lua:

  struct point { int x; int y; }  →  { "x": 1, "y": 2 }  →  { x=1, y=2 }  (Lua table)

 Lua → JSON → C requires the destination C type to be known (which it
   always is, because the receiving C box declared its input). The Lua
   spec's native_to_json walks the table and emits JSON; the receiving
   C box's wrapper parses the JSON into the typed struct it expects.
  Mismatches (Lua table missing a field, wrong type, extra fields) get
   rejected at unpack time with a precise error.

  Lua-specific values landing in C

  The hard cases you pointed at:

  - Lua function in a Lua table that crosses to C. Only meaningful if
  the receiving C field is a function pointer. SoraMech handles this
  via the callback rule: the user declares in the spec that this C
  field is a callback to a Lua function; the wrapper generator emits a
   C trampoline that captures a reference to the Lua function
  (registered in LUA_REGISTRYINDEX) and invokes it when called.
  - Lua-only types (coroutine, userdata, metatables): can't cross to
  C. The Lua spec's native_to_json refuses them with a clear error.
  The user moves them or restructures.

  So the rule is: Lua → C only succeeds if every Lua value has a C
  type that can receive it. Most do (numbers, strings,
  tables-as-structs, functions-as-callbacks). The exotic ones
  (coroutines, raw userdata) don't.

  Is infer_signature part of the spec?

  Yes — and you've pushed me to make it part of the spec contract
  uniformly. Every spec exposes:

  typedef struct {
      /* ... */
  } sm_signature_t;

  /* List the function names declared in `path` that this spec can
   * recognise. Editor uses this to populate the function-name
   * dropdown after the user picks a source file. */
  char **(*list_functions)(void *handle, const char *path, int
  *out_count);

  /* Return the inferred signature for `path::fn` plus any holes
   * the user needs to resolve. The editor uses this to render
   * input/output slots and to render the "param rules" form when
   * holes are present. */
  sm_signature_t *(*infer_signature)(void *handle,
                                     const char *path, const char
  *fn);

  Two more lang_spec_t callbacks. The C spec's implementation does the
   gcc--E-then-parse dance. The Lua spec inspects the file via the
  per-worker lua_State (find a returned function, check its
  debug.getinfo for parameter names, infer types from the
  user-declared annotations or sentinel-default heuristics). The Bash
  spec probably returns a trivial signature (every input is a string,
  return is a string) because bash functions don't carry types.

  This means the editor speaks one API to every language, and 307's
  parser becomes the C-side implementation of a contract that 317
  already shares. Both issues converge on the same spec extension.

  Updated sub-issue split

  Three changes drop out of this conversation:

  - 307a stays as "type system + serialization runtime." Now explicit
  about handling every C type form (primitives, pointer-to-T, arrays,
  structs, unions, enums, function pointers, typedefs).
  - 307b is "C signature parser via gcc -E + recursive-descent
  declarator parser." Returns sm_signature_t with explicit
  hole-markers for un-serializable params.
  - 307c is "wrapper code generator." Consumes sm_signature_t plus the
   user's spec config (param_rules) and emits the bridge .c.
  - 307d is now "the list_functions / infer_signature extension to
  lang_spec_t, and the C spec's implementation of them." This is the
  editor-facing API.

  Plus a meta-note: 307d's spec extension is also what 317 needs. The
  two issues converge — 317 adds native_to_json / json_to_native for
  cross-language data flow, and 307d adds list_functions /
  infer_signature for editor introspection. They're four callbacks
  landing in the same spec interface, on parallel tracks.

  Want me to start writing 307a as a sub-issue blueprint now, with the
   design points from this conversation woven in?

hi, can you read this and get accustomed to working with the stuff it mentions? our goal today is to build a design that doesn't have any unserializable parts. We should be able to write a generic interpreter that reads code in any language and signals to the user that they need to build an edge case for it into the spec. Please help me design a system that accomplishes this. I will judge.

RE pointers without a length: we need to keep track of memory, and how much has been allocated when pointers are assigned. So for example if we make a long string, we separately need to keep track of it's length for management of the heap. This is unfortunate side effects of having dynamic systems, sometimes you have to do things twice. In this case, the library is not performant with our system, and we're doing a "compatibility" mode to help it run at all.

  So the rule is: Lua → C only succeeds if every Lua value has a C
  type that can receive it. Most do (numbers, strings,
  tables-as-structs, functions-as-callbacks). The exotic ones
  (coroutines, raw userdata) don't.

For those, we need to break them down until we truly understand what they're comprised of. Type deconstruction and decomposition, then re-arranged into something we can actually use. It's just data at the end of the day, so we need to be able to transfer seamlessly. We shouldn't have specific cases for coroutines or raw userdata or whatever - they should just be another case that the user defines in the spec. We should be able to have as many as we need, and it crucially needs to be language agnostic - the lua spec can't know ANYTHING about the C or bash spec. They must be agnostic.

## Assistant

Reading the memory now. Good — I'm aligned on the project shape. Here's where I land after re-reading your message.

## Unions — not a tuple

A tuple is a *product type*: all fields exist at once, each at its own offset. A C `struct point { int x; int y; }` is a tuple — `x` and `y` both live, both readable.

A union is a *sum type*: all fields share the same bytes. Only one is "live" at a time. The classic shape:

```c
union value { int i; float f; char *s; };  /* 8 bytes, one of three meanings */
```

You write `v.i = 42`, then read `v.f`, and you get the float interpretation of `0x0000002a` — garbage, because you reinterpreted the bytes. Unions are almost always paired with a discriminator field that says "which variant is currently live":

```c
struct tagged {
    enum { INT, FLOAT, STR } tag;
    union { int i; float f; char *s; } v;
};
```

The reason this matters for serialization: a bare union has no way to tell you which variant to serialize. Three different fields lay claim to the same bytes. Without a discriminator we'd have to pick arbitrarily. With a discriminator we're fine — read the tag, serialize that variant.

So at the spec layer, bare unions become another "user provides a rule" case — same shape as length-unknown pointers. The rule says either "field X is the discriminator" or "this union is always variant Y in this context." The system never guesses.

## The core design — handles as the universal fallback

Your push to make this language-agnostic with no special cases reframes the whole thing. Let me state what I think you're actually proposing, because it's a cleaner design than what I had:

**Every value in every language factors into exactly one of two cases:**

1. **Decomposable.** The spec can break it down into a tree of primitives (numbers, strings, bytes, bools, plus ordered lists and named records and tagged variants). Round-trips natively to any other spec.

2. **Handle.** The spec can't (or won't) decompose it. Instead it emits a *handle* — a tiny primitive token (an id string plus a list of operations it supports). The handle crosses the wire. The underlying state stays in the originating language. When some other language wants to act on the handle, the runtime routes the operation back to the owning spec.

That's the whole serialization rule. No third case. **Nothing is unserializable, because the worst case is "this becomes a handle."**

A Lua coroutine? Handle. The Lua spec owns the coroutine; the handle says `ops: ["resume","status","close"]`. C never sees the call stack — it just calls `invoke_handle(id, "resume", args)` and the runtime dispatches back to Lua.

A C function pointer to unknown code? Handle. The C spec owns the pointer; the handle says `ops: ["call"]` plus the signature (if known). Lua never dereferences — it asks the runtime to invoke, and the C spec does the actual call.

A C pointer-to-opaque-struct? Handle. The struct stays on the C heap. C-side accessors are the `ops`.

This works because *no language ever has to understand another language's exotic types*. Every spec only ever sees primitives, composites of primitives, or handles. Handles are also primitives (just a string id with op metadata). The spec interface is uniform.

## Teaching the system to decompose

The handle is the safe default. But sometimes you *want* a type to be transparent — you want a Lua userdata to flow into C as a real struct, not as an opaque token. That's where user-written rules come in:

Each spec exposes a **rule registry**. The user writes rules in the spec's own native form (Lua rules for Lua types, C rules for C types) and registers them. A rule says "for values of type T, here's how to decompose to a tree, and here's how to rebuild from a tree." Once the rule exists, the spec's `to_tree` uses it instead of falling back to a handle.

This means:
- The Lua spec ships with built-in rules for the easy types (number, string, table-as-record, table-as-list).
- The user writes rules for their own exotic types (a custom userdata class, a coroutine they want decomposed into call-stack metadata).
- The C spec ships with rules for primitives, structs, arrays, length-tracked pointers.
- The user writes rules for the awkward C cases (length-unknown pointers via a length hint, unions via a discriminator hint, opaque pointers via accessor functions).

The Lua spec doesn't know what C does. The C spec doesn't know what Lua does. Both produce the same tree format, both consume the same tree format. The runtime is dumb glue.

## Compatibility mode — the C parallel allocation tracker

Right — when a C library returns a pointer without length info, we shim around it. The C spec owns an **allocation table**: pointer → (length, owning-fn-or-rule, freed-flag). Every pointer that flows out of a C box gets an entry. Length comes from one of:

- The function's documented behavior (rule says "return value's length is param 3")
- A heuristic the user opted into (rule says "null-terminated")
- A wrapper around the C call that captured the length at allocation time (rule says "wrap malloc/strdup/etc. and record the size")

If none of those apply and the user hasn't written a rule — the pointer becomes a handle. The user gets a UI prompt: "this output has a length-unknown pointer; add a rule to make it transparent." Two clicks, type a rule, done.

You called this "doing things twice" — yes, exactly. The C library doesn't tell us the length; we have to track it separately. The cost is paid once, in the wrapper layer, and only for libraries that don't carry length info themselves.

## What I'd like to go over with you

A few things genuinely puzzle me about this design, and I want your call before I commit to anything:

1. **Handle lifetime.** A Lua coroutine becomes a handle that flows to C. C stores it in some structure for an hour. When does Lua garbage-collect the underlying coroutine? Options I see:

   - **Refcount in the runtime.** Every box that holds a handle increments; when the box's slot is overwritten or the run ends, decrement; at zero, runtime calls `release_handle` on the owning spec. Simple, but it means *every* handle-touch on every wire is a refcount op.
   - **Pin until end of run.** Handles live until the run is over; spec releases everything then. Wasteful for long runs but trivially correct.
   - **Generation tags.** Each run has a generation; handles carry the generation; the runtime sweeps releasable handles between runs. Cheap during the run, complex at boundaries.

   Do you have a preference, or want me to try option 2 first as a baseline and only complicate if it bites us?

2. **Handle equality.** If two boxes both produce a handle to the same underlying Lua coroutine, should the handles compare equal? (Same id.) That requires the spec's `to_tree` to maintain a "object → id" map so it returns the same id for the same object. Cheap on the spec side (a pointer-keyed table), but it's a design choice — do we want this invariant? I'd argue yes, because otherwise you can't tell whether two outputs are the same coroutine or two different ones.

3. **The recursion question.** A Lua table contains 9 known fields and 1 unknown userdata. Do we (a) emit a handle for the whole table, or (b) emit a record where 9 fields are decomposed and the 10th is a nested handle? I'd push for (b) — decompose maximally, leave handles only where the spec genuinely can't proceed. That makes user-rule-writing incremental: each rule eliminates one handle, the rest of the structure was already useful. Agree?

4. **Where rules live.** Per-spec, in a file under `langs/<name>/rules/`, written in the spec's native syntax? Or per-map, so each map can have its own type vocabulary? Or both — global default rules plus per-map overrides? My instinct says per-spec global with per-map overrides, but I haven't thought hard about it.

5. **The tagged-variant primitive.** The tree format needs four leaf-and-shape kinds: primitive (number/string/bytes/bool/null), ordered list, named record, *and* a tagged variant (for sum types — `{"tag": "INT", "value": 42}`). That fourth one is what we need to round-trip C unions and Rust enums and Lua "table-as-variant" patterns. Sound right? I want to make sure we have product *and* sum types in the tree, because without sum types we'd be unable to represent any tagged union losslessly.

6. **Does a handle have a "type name"?** I'm thinking yes — the handle carries `{id, owner_lang, type_name, ops}`. The type_name is what the user references when writing a rule ("for handles of type Lua.coroutine, decompose like this"). Without a type_name there's no way for the user to target a rule at a category of handles.

## Assistant

You're right. Let me re-articulate, because I was tangled.

## Conceding handles

The argument that broke it for me: a real library function (`qsort`, `pthread_create`, anything from libc) expects a real native value. It can't accept a SoraMech-id-string and route operations back via the runtime — it has no notion of SoraMech and never will. If our system can't hand a real `int (*)(const void*, const void*)` to qsort, the whole story falls apart for library integration. So handles as a wire format are out.

## What the design actually is

Every value, no matter how exotic, decomposes to a tree of primitives. That tree is the wire format. The destination spec reconstructs the value as a real native value of whatever type its declared input signature says — *including* synthesizing native executables (trampolines, FFI bridges) when the destination expects something callable.

The wire only ever carries data. The "handle"-shaped thing — a callable pointer whose body routes calls back to the originating worker — is reconstructed *at receive time* by the destination spec, transparently. From the library function's perspective: it sees a normal function pointer. From the wire's perspective: it carried `{address_in_source_worker, signature, owner_worker_id}` as primitives. The destination C spec's reconstruction step, given that tree and the declared C type "function pointer with signature (int,int)->int", emits a libffi-generated trampoline that, when called by qsort, marshals args back across to the originating worker. The trampoline IS the reconstructed value.

Same trick for Lua coroutines into C: the source Lua spec decomposes the coroutine into its parts (bytecode of frames, locals, upvalues, status, pc) — pure data. If the C box's input signature is `struct lua_frame[]`, the C spec walks the tree and fills the struct. If the C box's input signature is "callable that returns next value" — C spec emits a trampoline that ships resume calls back to the source Lua worker.

What this means: **every concrete value the user wires up is a real, callable, dereferenceable, native value in the destination language.** The wire is just bytes; the destination spec does whatever it needs to make those bytes into the native form its signature demands.

## "The user won't know the edge cases"

Right — and the answer is: **the user doesn't have to.** The spec author handles every type form the language has. The Lua spec ships knowing how to decompose: number, string, bool, nil, table, function, userdata, thread/coroutine, lightuserdata. *Every* Lua type form. The C spec ships knowing how to handle primitives, structs, unions (when they have a discriminator), arrays (when they have a length), function pointers (when they have a signature), enums, typedefs. *Every* C type form.

What the user might have to fill in is **per-library-function** ambiguities — things the C type system can't express but the library's documentation does. Three of them:

- Union with no discriminator field declared in the same struct → user marks which other param carries the tag.
- Pointer with no length field declared in the same struct → user marks which other param carries the length (or marks the pointer as null-terminated, or as "single element").
- Function pointer parameter typed as bare `void *` → user provides the signature.

These three are not "edge cases the user has to discover." They're **prompts surfaced by the editor** the first time the user wires up a library function whose signature parser couldn't fully resolve. The editor renders the box with a red "type hole" slot. The user clicks, fills in the answer ("discriminator is in param 2"), saves. The answer stores as an annotation on that library function name + header path, lives in `langs/c/library-annotations/`, ships back across runs and machines. Next user who wires up the same function: no prompt, annotation already there. First-party libc functions ship with annotations pre-filled.

So the user is never expected to *think about* what an ambiguous case might be. The editor tells them, in context, when one occurs. Three categories total, finite and learnable.

## The per-call vs. per-function vs. per-type question

You asked: "How else would we mark a certain argument to the function as the discriminator?" My answer is per-function, not per-call-site, not per-type:

- **Per-type** doesn't work — same union appears in many functions, each with a different discriminator convention.
- **Per-call-site** is overkill — the same function called twice in a graph has the same discriminator semantics.
- **Per-function** is the right granularity. The annotation attaches to "function `foo` declared in header `<bar.h>`." Once written, it applies everywhere `foo` is used.

The annotations live in the C spec's library-annotations directory. They are part of the spec, not the map. A compiled map artifact carries no spec knowledge — it just carries the graph, and depends on the destination machine having the relevant specs and annotations.

## Where there's still tension

Two cases I want you to weigh in on before I treat them as settled:

1. **Cross-process function pointers.** Lua-and-C are in-process (`dlsym`, `lua_pcall`). Bash is out-of-process (UDS to a per-worker subprocess). If a C box outputs a function pointer wired into a Bash box's input, what should happen? My instinct: this is incoherent — a Bash subprocess can't call into another process's address space, full stop. The compile step should detect "callable being wired into an out-of-process language" and error. Sound right, or do you see a way to make it work that I'm missing?

2. **A Lua coroutine fully decomposed → received by C.** We can decompose the coroutine to its data parts (frames, locals, upvalues, pc, status). The C box that receives it... can do what with it? If the C box's signature is `struct lua_coroutine_snapshot { ... }`, the C spec fills the struct from the tree and C reads it as data. Fine — that's introspection. But if the C box wants to *resume* the coroutine, the only way is to ship resume calls back to the Lua side. The destination spec's reconstruction would have to synthesize a "resumeable handle" — which is basically the handle pattern I was conceding, just localized to the destination spec. Does that count as a violation of "no handles"? I think the distinction is: the wire carried only data, and the destination spec chose to wrap that data in a callable bridge to make it useful. It's a destination-side artifact, not a wire concern. Agree?

3. **One thing I want to confirm about your "no handles" position.** A Lua function being passed as a callback into C is the same shape as #2 above. The function decomposes to `{bytecode, upvalues, ...}` — data. The C spec receives that tree and synthesizes a real C function pointer (libffi trampoline) that, when called, marshals args back to the originating Lua worker, executes the function there, marshals the return back. From qsort's perspective: real function pointer. From the wire's perspective: just data crossed. From the C spec's perspective: a trampoline was synthesized, and it owns a back-reference to the source worker. Is this fine, or are you also pushing back on the "back-reference" part? Because without some back-reference, there's no way for the trampoline to know where to send the call when it gets invoked.

Where I think we are: the wire is pure data, the destination spec reconstructs into real native values, and any "phone home" mechanism for callbacks is private to the destination spec's reconstruction artifact. The wire stays agnostic; the bridges live in the specs. Does that match what you're driving at?

We should try and use shared memory as much as possible, in fact we need to use it for everything that we can. That system requires that we use shared memory that all threads can access, and that they're immutable (except by the one who has the authority to write there, which is just whoever has a wire coming in to the receiving box, of which there might be several into the same input port, in which case the inputted values are added to a ring buffer and provided to each separate enque of that particular box)

## Assistant

You're right on all three pushbacks, and the shared-memory point reframes the whole design. Let me state where we are.

## The shift you just made

The wire isn't JSON-of-data. The wire is a slot holding **a pointer + a type-descriptor**, where the pointer references shared heap and the descriptor tells the receiver how to interpret bytes there. JSON is only the fallback at cross-process boundaries (anything ↔ Bash). For in-process work (Lua ↔ C, our common case), we pass pointers and descriptors — nothing is "moved," everything is "looked at."

That fits the slot store you already designed for the threading model: per-input-port shared cells, immutable-once-written, ring-buffered. Wires populate cells with `{ptr, length, type_descriptor}`. Receiving boxes walk the bytes through the descriptor.

So your big-string worry — "Would we send the entire text as an argument? that seems dumb!" — yes that would be dumb, and we don't. The text sits where it was malloced. The slot holds `{ptr, length, "bytes"}`. The Lua box that wants it gets the pointer. If Lua wants it as a normal string, the Lua spec does `lua_pushlstring(L, ptr, length)` and yes that copies into Lua's own heap (Lua owns its string memory, no way around that without breaking Lua semantics). If Lua just wants to read it without copying, the Lua spec offers a "lightuserdata + length" reception mode — Lua holds the raw pointer, reads bytes directly. The user picks per box. Either way the *wire* didn't move the text.

## The discriminator / vocab problem dissolves

You're right that "discriminator" is a vocab word the system shouldn't impose. Here's the cleaner shape:

**The destination's signature drives interpretation.** Source emits bytes + descriptor of what it produced. Destination has its own declared input type. The wire compile checks: can the source's shape be coerced to the destination's shape?

- C union → Lua int input: Lua spec reads the first 4 bytes as int. If C wrote the float variant, Lua reads garbage. That's a user-made incoherent graph, exactly as you said — they fix it with a translation box or by adjusting source code. We don't ask about discriminators; we just let the destination's signature pick the interpretation.
- Pointer with no length → destination expects a string: receiving spec walks null-terminated. Destination expects a sized buffer: wire compile fails with "destination needs length, source doesn't provide one — wire a length input here, or change the source."
- Function pointer typed `void *` at source → destination declares the actual signature: receiving spec uses the destination's signature to do the FFI cast.

In every case the user is never asked "what's the discriminator?" The destination signature already says. If source and destination can't be coerced, the compile fails *at the wire*, with a specific fix-it message, in vocabulary the user already knows ("you need a length here," "this signature doesn't match," "wire something into this slot").

## What the three wire paths actually are

a. **Same-language in-process**: language-native bytes via shared heap. Lua-to-Lua uses `lua_dump` blobs; C-to-C uses struct memcpy. Already what issue 312 set up.

b. **Cross-language in-process** (Lua ↔ C): pointer + type-descriptor via shared heap. Receiving spec walks the descriptor against the bytes, building its native value. Copies only where the destination language requires owned memory.

c. **Cross-process** (any ↔ Bash): the marshaller at the language boundary serializes (using the same descriptor) into JSON-on-stdin. Slow but works across address spaces. This is also where some "in-process trick" wires legitimately fail — e.g. a function pointer can't flow into a Bash subprocess, and the compile must catch it.

## Where we are

The design now is: **shared memory + type descriptors + destination-driven interpretation**. No JSON on hot paths. No handles. No discriminator vocab in user-facing prompts. No up-front "user must annotate" step — annotations only appear when the wire compile spots a coercion gap, and it shows the gap in plain-English terms tied to the destination's expectation. Specs ship knowing how to walk their language's full type system against a descriptor; the user only fills in answers the system can't infer (always specific, always prompted in context).

The one open thing I want to put back to you: **the type descriptor itself.** It needs a shape that every spec can both produce (decomposing its native types) and consume (interpreting bytes at a pointer). Something like:

- `{kind: "primitive", form: "int32" | "float64" | "bytes" | ...}`
- `{kind: "struct", fields: [{name, offset, descriptor}], size}`
- `{kind: "array", element_descriptor, length_source: <inline | wire | sentinel | null-terminated>}`
- `{kind: "function_pointer", signature: <descriptor of return + params>}`
- `{kind: "union", variants: [...]}` — let the destination pick
- `{kind: "opaque", lang: "lua", form: "coroutine"}` — for things genuinely not byte-introspectable from outside the owning language; destination spec decides what to do

That last one is the only place where the source spec "gives up" and tells the destination "this is a Lua-internal structure, you handle it or I'll route it back." It's not a handle in the runtime sense — it's just a hint in the descriptor that says "the bytes here are only meaningful to a Lua spec." A Lua destination uses its built-in knowledge; a non-Lua destination either fails the wire compile or invokes a user-written translation box.

Sound like the right next thing to nail down? If yes — I'd write up the descriptor format as the next concrete piece, and that becomes the basis for the parser (307b) and the wrapper generator (307c).

## Assistant

Both corrections land. Restating where we are.

## JSON always, shared memory as a JSON primitive

You're right — pair-wise paths break the moment we add a fourth language. The discipline is: **every spec exposes only `to_json` and `from_json`**. JSON is the universal intermediate; the source spec never knows who's downstream, the destination never knows who emitted.

But JSON does not have to mean "encode every byte into a string." JSON can carry a primitive form like:

```json
{"$ref": {"ptr": 140234..., "len": 1048576, "type": "bytes"}}
```

The bytes themselves stay in shared heap. The JSON carries the reference. Receiving spec sees `$ref`, decides what to do with it: C reads at the pointer directly; Lua does `lua_pushlstring(L, ptr, len)` (which copies, because Lua owns its string memory); Bash subprocess can't dereference a pointer from another address space, so the marshaller at the UDS boundary inlines the bytes into the JSON before sending. The decision lives in each spec's `from_json`, not in the wire format. The wire is JSON, end of story; the `$ref` primitive is just one of the things JSON can carry.

This means specs stay completely decoupled. Adding a fifth language is: implement `to_json` and `from_json`. Done.

## The length-pairing question dissolves

You're right that we can't know which int is "the length." A signature `void foo(char *p, int n)` is two independent parameters as far as the type system is concerned. The convention "n is length of p" lives in the function's documentation, not its types. We have no way to infer.

So we don't. The C box for `foo` has two inputs: a `char *` slot and an `int` slot. They're separate wires. The user reads the function's docs and wires accordingly — `p` from one source, `n` from another (or from a `strlen`-style box, or as a literal). The system never asks "is this the length?" — it just exposes both as inputs and lets the user wire them.

Same shape for unions: if the C signature has a variant and a tag, those are two inputs. The user wires them coherently. If they wire them incoherently (tag says INT, variant has float bytes), garbage at runtime — user's incoherent graph, same as you said.

This kills per-function annotations entirely. No vocab words to learn. No "discriminator" prompt. The function's signature is what the function's signature is, and the user wires its inputs the same way they'd write its call site in C. The editor surfaces the slots; the user fills them with wires.

## No back-routing — confirmed

Specs are self-contained. If a destination spec's `from_json` can't interpret the JSON it receives, the call errors and the run aborts (hard-crash policy). If no wire consumes a value, the slot just expires — the source-language GC reclaims whatever was there. No "phone home." No special handling.

This means: a C function pointer can flow into Lua because Lua-the-spec knows FFI and can `from_json` a `$function_pointer` primitive into a callable. The same pointer flowing into Bash fails at compile time because Bash-the-spec's `from_json` declares it doesn't accept `$function_pointer`. A Lua coroutine into C either works (if the C box declares an input type the Lua spec's `to_json` decomposition fits into) or fails at compile time. No magic to make incoherent wires work.

## Where we are

The spec interface, minimal:

- `to_json(native_value, output_signature) → json` — produces a JSON value (with `$ref` / `$function_pointer` / similar primitives as needed) representing the native value.
- `from_json(json, input_signature) → native_value` — reconstructs the native value, copying or aliasing per the language's needs.
- `list_functions(path) → [function_names]` — editor introspection.
- `infer_signature(path, fn) → signature_tree` — editor introspection, with all parameters exposed as separate slots (no inference about which param "is" the length of another).
- plus existing `init`, `call`, `shutdown`.

Per-spec primitives that need to live in the JSON spec: `$ref` (shared-memory bytes), `$function_pointer` (address + signature, only meaningful in-process), maybe `$lang_opaque` (a tag saying "these bytes are Lua-internal" so non-Lua specs cleanly fail at compile rather than silently mis-interpreting). The set is small and finite — it grows only when a language genuinely needs a new representation form that JSON-natives can't carry.

I'd write up the JSON primitive format as the next concrete piece, and that becomes the contract every spec implements `to_json` / `from_json` against. Sound good as the next step?

77	**actually implement them**, driven by the data-box use case.
78	(312 needs them too, but its driver is performance; 317 needs
79	them for correctness — a data box can't function without them.)
80	
81	### The rule, restated
82	
83	The author of a data box writes a `.lua` / `.c` / `.sh` /
84	`.<whatever>` file that returns or produces a value using
85	*their language's native idioms*. No SoraMech macros. No
86	SoraMech includes. No SoraMech-specific JSON shape constraints
87	beyond "what your spec's native_to_json can convert."
88	
89	If the author returns a Lua table, the Lua spec's
90	`native_to_json` converts it. If they return a C struct, the C
91	spec's `native_to_json` walks it according to the type declared
92	in the box. If they print JSON-ish text from a bash script, the
93	bash spec's `native_to_json` is a no-op identity (the script
94	already produced JSON).
95	
96	The contract belongs to the spec author, not the box author.
97	That's the inversion: **SoraMech bends to the language, not the
98	other way around.**
99	
100	## Native handle representation
101	
102	The shape of `src` / `dst` in the callbacks is per-spec. Each
103	spec documents what it accepts:
104	
105	- **Lua spec**: `src` is the index of a value already pushed
106	  onto the worker's `lua_State`. `src_size` is unused. The
107	  spec's `native_to_json` reads the value at that stack index
108	  and writes JSON. `json_to_native` pushes the parsed JSON value
109	  onto the stack, and `dst_size` reports the number of stack
110	  slots used (always 1 for SoraMech's flat-value model).
111	- **C spec**: `src` is a typed pointer + the box's declared
112	  output type drives the walk. `src_size` is the type's
113	  storage footprint. Mirror image for `json_to_native`. (This
114	  ties into 307's typed-wrapper-generation work — the same
115	  type system describes both the wrapper signature and the
116	  JSON shape.)
117	- **Bash spec**: `src` is text the script produced.
118	  `native_to_json` validates and reshapes to JSON if needed (a
119	  bash script that prints `42` becomes JSON `42`; a script that
120	  prints `{"a":1}` passes through unchanged). `json_to_native`
121	  is similarly a near-identity. The spec author is encouraged
122	  to require scripts produce well-formed JSON in the first
123	  place rather than write a bash-syntax parser.
124	
125	The dispatch layer doesn't know or care which representation a
126	spec uses. It only ever passes `src` and `src_size` to the
127	spec's callback. The spec is opaque to the runtime; that's the
128	whole point.
129	
130	## Data box semantics
131	
132	A data box's `data` field becomes optional. The box gains
133	optional `lang` and `ref` fields:
134	
135	```json
136	{
137	  "id": "config",
138	  "kind": "data",
139	  "lang": "lua",
140	  "ref": "config.lua"
141	}
142	```
143	
144	At graph load, the runner:
145	
146	1. Resolves the spec for `lang`.
147	2. Calls the spec's `init` (already done at worker startup).
148	3. At box-fire time, asks the spec to execute the `ref` file
149	   and produce a native value (this is essentially the same
150	   "load this file, run it, get its return value" path the
151	   spec already implements for call boxes — call boxes call a
152	   named function; data boxes take the file's return value).
153	4. Calls the spec's `native_to_json` to convert the native
154	   value to JSON text.
155	5. Pushes that JSON text down the wire as the box's output —
156	   same as today's data path, just with the value sourced
157	   from a language-native file.
158	
159	If the box has no `lang` and a literal `data` field, the legacy
160	inline-data path applies unchanged.
161	
162	## Why every spec must implement these
163	
164	The same JSON the data box emits is consumed by some downstream
165	box (call, comparator, file_write, whatever). That downstream
166	box, if it's in a different language, will eventually need
167	*its* spec's `json_to_native` to convert the JSON back. So the
168	two callbacks are paired — every spec that participates in the
169	graph needs both. A spec that implements `native_to_json` but
170	not `json_to_native` can only be data-box sources; a spec with
171	only `json_to_native` can only be call-box sinks. We require
172	both so any language can play any role.
173	
174	Specs that haven't been updated yet should set both callbacks
175	to a stub that returns -1 with a clear error message ("spec
176	`<name>`: native_to_json not implemented; this box's value
177	can't be routed cross-language"). The hard-fail makes the gap
178	loud rather than producing wrong output.
179	
180	## Suggested implementation steps
181	
182	1. Extend the `lang_spec_t` declaration in
183	   `langs/lang-spec.h` if not already done by 312's
184	   infrastructure work. (Per the round summary, the slot is
185	   reserved.)
186	2. Implement `native_to_json` and `json_to_native` in each
187	   spec:
188	   - **Lua spec**: walk the value at the stack index. Use the
189	     existing C JSON writer (`libs/json/json.c`). Handle Lua
190	     tables both as arrays (1-indexed contiguous integer keys)
191	     and as objects. Refuse functions, userdata, threads.
192	   - **C spec**: depends on 307's typed-wrapper machinery. For
193	     now, a stub that only supports scalar types (int, double,
194	     string) is enough to get data boxes working in C.
195	   - **Bash spec**: validate-and-pass-through. The script is
196	     expected to produce JSON. `native_to_json` runs the JSON
197	     parser to validate; `json_to_native` is identity.
198	3. Extend the data box runtime path in `src/012-dispatch.c`
199	   (or wherever data boxes dispatch from) to consult `lang` /
200	   `ref` when set and route through the spec callback chain.
... [truncated; 200-line cap]
```

1	# 307 — C language spec implementation
2	
3	## Status
4	open
5	
6	## Current behavior
7	C boxes today run via `drivers/c.sh`, which compiles the source on
8	demand (mtime-based recompile to a binary), then spawns the binary as
9	a subprocess and reads stdout. Process spawn cost dominates; each call
10	is its own freshly-launched executable. The phase 2 driver compiles
11	to a standalone binary, not a `.so`.
12	
13	## Concept
14	The C language spec implements `lang_spec_t` (issue 303) for C. It is
15	the second reference implementation, and the first one that exercises
16	the `compile` callback. After compile, invocation is in-process via
17	`dlopen` / `dlsym` — the user's compiled function is called directly
18	through a function pointer, with no subprocess and no IPC.
19	
20	This is the "Option 1 — LuaJIT FFI" model from
21	`docs/004-ipc-and-threading.md`, applied to C: same-process call,
22	nanosecond-overhead invocation, native-typed access. The only thing
23	the C spec adds over the Lua spec is the compile step.
24	
25	## Per-worker state
26	
27	`init` returns a small struct holding the dlopen-handle cache:
28	
29	```c
30	typedef struct {
31	    void  *so_handles[MAX_BOXES];   // per-file .so handle, indexed by file id
32	    void  *fn_ptrs   [MAX_BOXES];   // per-(file, fn) cached function pointer
33	    int    n_cached;
34	    // (a hash table could replace these arrays if MAX_BOXES grows large)
35	} c_handle_t;
36	
37	void *c_init(int worker_idx);
38	void  c_teardown(void *handle);    // dlclose every cached handle
39	```
40	
41	Each worker thread has its own cache. The same `.so` will end up
42	loaded once per worker, since `dlopen` with the same path on the same
43	process returns a refcounted handle (the underlying code segment is
44	shared across all callers — the per-worker `dlopen` is just a
45	refcount bump after the first).
46	
47	## Compile callback
48	
49	The C spec's `compile` callback receives the box's metadata in
50	addition to the source and output paths, so it knows the function's
51	signature and can generate the wrapper:
52	
53	```c
54	int c_compile(const char *src_path, const char *out_path, const box_t *box);
55	```
56	
57	(This is a small extension to `lang_spec_t` in issue 303 — `compile`
58	gains a `box_t` pointer. Lua's compile is NULL, so unaffected.)
59	
60	The callback:
61	1. Reads `box->inputs`, `box->returns`, `box->headers`, `box->link_libs`,
62	   `box->cflags` from the box metadata.
63	2. Generates a wrapper `.c` file (see "Wrapper generation" below) in
64	   the map's `compiled/bin/` directory next to where the `.so` will
65	   land.
66	3. Forks and execs the system `cc` with:
67	   - `-shared -fPIC -O2 -Wall` (defaults)
68	   - The user's `cflags`, if any
69	   - The wrapper `.c` and the user's `src_path` (if `ref` is set)
70	   - `-o out_path`
71	   - `-l<lib>` for each entry in `box->link_libs`
72	4. Waits for the compiler, returns 0 on success, 1 on failure.
73	
74	If the resulting `.so` is newer than the user's source and the
75	wrapper, compile is skipped (mtime check, same as phase 2).
76	
77	The compiler is `cc` — the system default. `gcc` on most Linux,
78	`clang` on macOS. No SoraMech-specific compiler dependency.
79	
80	### cflags in box JSON
81	The `cflags` field on the box JSON is a string of additional compiler
82	flags (`-O0 -g`, `-mavx2`, etc.). It is set by the user in the
83	inspector — a "Compile flags" text input shown when a C-language box
84	is selected. The editor saves it as part of the box's JSON; reads
85	preserve the field; saves write it back. Nothing overwrites it.
86	
87	## Box file convention: any C function, no boilerplate
88	
89	**The rule, stated as a rule:** a box author writes code as if
90	SoraMech does not exist. The function they write must be a
91	function their non-SoraMech compiler can compile, their
92	non-SoraMech editor can lint, their non-SoraMech test harness
93	can call. No SoraMech-prefixed macros. No SoraMech-shaped
94	return types. No `#include "soramech.h"`. Nothing in the
95	author's source betrays that the function will ever cross
96	SoraMech's wire.
97	
98	SoraMech bends to the language. The language never bends to
99	SoraMech. This is non-negotiable across every spec; it's the
100	reason for the spec interface in the first place.
101	
102	Users do not write a SoraMech-specific C signature. They reference
103	any C function — whether one they wrote themselves, or one from a
104	library (zlib, OpenCV, libcurl, anything they have headers for) —
105	and the C spec generates a thin wrapper that bridges SoraMech's
106	bytes-in / bytes-out ABI to the function's natural typed C
107	signature.
108	
109	### What the user provides
110	Either:
111	- A `.c` file containing the function, referenced by `ref` in the
112	  box JSON; or
113	- A library function from a header, no `.c` file of their own — the
114	  `ref` field is omitted, the function name and headers are
115	  declared in the box JSON, and the library is named in
116	  `link_libs`.
117	
118	### Box JSON for a user-written function
119	
120	```json
121	{
122	  "id": "classify",
123	  "kind": "call",
124	  "lang": "c",
125	  "ref": "classify.c",
126	  "fn": "classify",
127	  "inputs": [
128	    {"name": "text",   "type": "string"},
129	    {"name": "weight", "type": "int"}
130	  ],
131	  "returns": "int"
132	}
133	```
134	
135	The user's `.c` file is naturally written:
136	
137	```c
138	// maps/<name>/src/classify.c
139	int classify(const char *text, int weight) {
140	    if (text[0] >= '0' && text[0] <= '9') return weight * 1;
141	    return weight * 2;
142	}
143	```
144	
145	No header to include, no macro, no `int classify(SM_BOX_ARGS)`.
146	Just a function with a typed signature.
147	
148	### Box JSON for a library function (no user source)
149	
150	```json
151	{
152	  "id": "compress",
153	  "kind": "call",
154	  "lang": "c",
155	  "fn": "compress2",
156	  "headers": ["<zlib.h>"],
157	  "link_libs": ["z"],
158	  "inputs": [
159	    {"name": "src",   "type": "bytes"},
160	    {"name": "level", "type": "int"}
161	  ],
162	  "returns": "bytes"
163	}
164	```
165	
166	The wrapper includes `<zlib.h>`, calls `compress2`, and the linker
167	pulls in `libz` via `-lz`. No user-written `.c` file.
168	
169	### Wrapper generation
170	
171	The compile callback emits a wrapper `.c` for each C box. The
172	wrapper has a fixed entry-point name (`sm_box_entry`) that the spec
173	always `dlsym`s. It declares the user's function (extern), unpacks
174	SoraMech's input bytes into typed C arguments per the box's `inputs`
175	declaration, calls the user's function, and writes the return value
176	into `sm_out_buf` per the box's `returns` declaration.
177	
178	For `classify` above, the generated wrapper is roughly:
179	
180	```c
181	// generated: classify_wrap.c
182	extern int classify(const char *, int);
183	
184	int sm_box_entry(const void **inputs, const int *sizes, int n,
185	                 void *out_buf, int out_cap, int *out_size) {
186	    const char *text   = (const char *)inputs[0];
187	    int         weight = atoi((const char *)inputs[1]);
188	    int         result = classify(text, weight);
189	    *out_size = snprintf(out_buf, out_cap, "%d", result);
190	    return 0;
191	}
192	```
193	
194	The wrapper is mechanical — same template for every box, varying
195	only in the input-marshalling lines and the return-encoding line.
196	The compile step writes it to `compiled/bin/<box-id>_wrap.c` and
197	compiles `<box-id>_wrap.c` plus the user's source (if any) into a
198	single `.so`.
199	
200	This means the C spec implements typed function calls without
... [truncated; 200-line cap]
```

/* langs/lang-spec.h — public interface for SoraMech language specs.
 *
 * What it is, in a sentence: the C contract that every language
 * spec (Lua, C, Bash, anything user-added) implements. The pool
 * runner discovers specs by dlopen()ing langs/<name>/spec.so at
 * startup and reading the exported soramech_lang_spec symbol.
 *
 * Designed in issue 303. The init barrier (issue 301) calls init()
 * for every spec on every worker before unblocking task dispatch;
 * the dispatch action (issue 304) calls invoke() per task; the
 * compile-button pipeline (issue 309) calls compile() per source
 * file for languages whose compile pointer is non-NULL.
 *
 * Errors: a nonzero return from any callback is fatal. The dispatch
 * layer prints the error context and aborts. There is no recovery,
 * no retry, no per-task failed state. This is intentional.
 *
 * Issue 312 extends this contract with optional fast-path
 * callbacks: `invoke_native` and `invoke_json` are specialised
 * variants the dispatch layer prefers when wire classification
 * says they apply; `native_to_json` and `json_to_native` are
 * bridges used at language boundaries. Specs that don't set them
 * fall back to `invoke` for every call.
 */

#ifndef SORAMECH_LANG_SPEC_H
#define SORAMECH_LANG_SPEC_H

#include <stddef.h>

#ifdef __cplusplus
extern "C" {
#endif

/* {{{ Shared signatures */
/* Invoke signature — shared by `invoke`, `invoke_native`, and
 * `invoke_json`. The dispatch layer picks which one to call based
 * on the wire-format classification in issue 312. */
typedef int (*lang_invoke_fn)(void *handle,
                              const char *file_path,
                              const char *fn_name,
                              const void **input_data,
                              const int   *input_sizes,
                              int          n_inputs,
                              void        *out_buf,
                              int          out_buf_capacity,
                              int         *out_size);

/* Wire-format bridge: convert one direction of native ↔ JSON.
 * Used when a value crosses a language boundary. Spec sets one
 * or both; if NULL, the dispatch layer treats native and JSON as
 * identical byte streams (the current uniform-byte default). */
typedef int (*lang_bridge_fn)(const void *src, int src_size,
                              void *dst, int dst_capacity, int *dst_size);
/* }}} */

/* {{{ lang_spec_t */
typedef struct lang_spec {
    const char *name;       /* "lua", "bash", "c", "python", ... */
    const char *file_ext;   /* ".lua", ".sh", ".c", ".py"        */

    /* init: once per worker thread at pool startup. Returns an
     * opaque handle stored in the worker context; the dispatch
     * layer passes this handle back to invoke() for every call. */
    void *(*init)(int worker_idx);

    /* teardown: once per worker at pool shutdown. Frees the handle
     * and any per-worker resources (e.g. lua_State, dlopen caches,
     * persistent socket connections). */
    void  (*teardown)(void *handle);

    /* compile: optional. Called once per source file at map-load
     * (or at editor compile-button time). For languages that don't
     * compile (Lua, Bash), leave this NULL. For C, it runs
     * gcc -shared -fPIC -o out_path src_path. Returns 0 on
     * success, nonzero on failure. */
    int   (*compile)(const char *src_path, const char *out_path);

    /* invoke: per box call. Inputs are arrays of (bytes, size) pairs,
     * already read from slots by the dispatch layer. Returns the
     * function's output by writing to out_buf and setting *out_size.
     * Returns 0 on success, nonzero (fatal) on error.
     *
     * This is the canonical entry point; every spec implements it.
     * The dispatch layer falls back to it when the specialised
     * paths below are not set. */
    lang_invoke_fn invoke;

    /* invoke_native — optional fast path used when every wire
     * adjacent to this box (input and output) stays inside this
     * spec's language. Bytes on the wire are this language's
     * native serialisation (Lua tables via msgpack, C struct
     * memcpy, etc.) and the encode/decode round trip is skipped.
     * NULL means the spec hasn't specialised; dispatch falls
     * back to `invoke`. Issue 312. */
    lang_invoke_fn invoke_native;

    /* invoke_json — optional universal-interop path. Bytes on the
     * wire are JSON. NULL means the spec hasn't specialised;
     * dispatch falls back to `invoke`. Issue 312. */
```

67	    /* teardown: once per worker at pool shutdown. Frees the handle
68	     * and any per-worker resources (e.g. lua_State, dlopen caches,
69	     * persistent socket connections). */
70	    void  (*teardown)(void *handle);
71	
72	    /* compile: optional. Called once per source file at map-load
73	     * (or at editor compile-button time). For languages that don't
74	     * compile (Lua, Bash), leave this NULL. For C, it runs
75	     * gcc -shared -fPIC -o out_path src_path. Returns 0 on
76	     * success, nonzero on failure. */
77	    int   (*compile)(const char *src_path, const char *out_path);
78	
79	    /* invoke: per box call. Inputs are arrays of (bytes, size) pairs,
80	     * already read from slots by the dispatch layer. Returns the
81	     * function's output by writing to out_buf and setting *out_size.
82	     * Returns 0 on success, nonzero (fatal) on error.
83	     *
84	     * This is the canonical entry point; every spec implements it.
85	     * The dispatch layer falls back to it when the specialised
86	     * paths below are not set. */
87	    lang_invoke_fn invoke;
88	
89	    /* invoke_native — optional fast path used when every wire
90	     * adjacent to this box (input and output) stays inside this
91	     * spec's language. Bytes on the wire are this language's
92	     * native serialisation (Lua tables via msgpack, C struct
93	     * memcpy, etc.) and the encode/decode round trip is skipped.
94	     * NULL means the spec hasn't specialised; dispatch falls
95	     * back to `invoke`. Issue 312. */
96	    lang_invoke_fn invoke_native;
97	
98	    /* invoke_json — optional universal-interop path. Bytes on the
99	     * wire are JSON. NULL means the spec hasn't specialised;
100	     * dispatch falls back to `invoke`. Issue 312. */
101	    lang_invoke_fn invoke_json;
102	
103	    /* Bridges between native and JSON wire forms. Used when a
104	     * value crosses a language boundary — the producer writes
105	     * native, the dispatch layer converts via the producer's
106	     * native_to_json, hands the JSON bytes to the consumer's
107	     * json_to_native. NULL means the spec hasn't specialised;
108	     * dispatch treats native and JSON as identical bytes. Issue
109	     * 312. */
110	    lang_bridge_fn native_to_json;
111	    lang_bridge_fn json_to_native;
112	} lang_spec_t;
113	/* }}} */
114	
115	#ifdef __cplusplus
116	}
117	#endif
118	
119	#endif /* SORAMECH_LANG_SPEC_H */

1	/* langs/lua/spec.c — Lua language spec for SoraMech.
2	 *
3	 * Per-worker handle is a `lua_State` with the standard libraries
4	 * opened. Invocation convention: the box's source file is expected
5	 * to return a Lua table (`return M` at the bottom), and the
6	 * fn_name is a key into that table. Inputs arrive as byte arrays;
7	 * each one is pushed as a Lua string. The function's single return
8	 * value is converted to a string (via tostring semantics) and
9	 * memcpy'd into the caller's output buffer.
10	 *
11	 * Designed in issue 306. Implementation choices that matter:
12	 *
13	 *  - One lua_State per worker thread. lua_State is not
14	 *    thread-safe, but per-thread requires no locking.
15	 *  - The state's stack is rewound to its baseline depth at every
16	 *    invoke entry/exit so cross-call leaks don't accumulate.
17	 *  - File caching (luaL_loadfile once, replayed via lua_call) is
18	 *    deferred — this iteration loads the chunk on every invoke.
19	 *    Lands as a follow-on once profiling justifies it.
20	 *  - The chunk's side effects run on every invoke too (since
21	 *    we re-load and re-execute). Box source files should be pure
22	 *    "return M" modules for now.
23	 */
24	
25	#include "lang-spec.h"
26	
27	#include <lua.h>
28	#include <lauxlib.h>
29	#include <lualib.h>
30	
31	#include <stdio.h>
32	#include <string.h>
33	
34	/* Registry key for the per-worker file → module-table cache. The
35	 * key is a string in the Lua registry; the value is a Lua table
36	 * keyed by file path. Each entry's value is the module table the
37	 * file's chunk returned.
38	 *
39	 * Caching is per-`lua_State` (i.e. per-worker), which matches the
40	 * thread model: each worker has its own state, never shared. No
41	 * locking needed because only the worker that owns the state ever
42	 * touches the cache. */
43	#define LUA_CACHE_KEY "soramech.module_cache"
44	
45	/* {{{ lua_init() — create a state, open standard libs, seed cache */
46	static void *lua_init(int worker_idx)
47	{
48	    (void)worker_idx;
49	    lua_State *L = luaL_newstate();
50	    if (!L) return NULL;
51	    luaL_openlibs(L);
52	    /* Seed the empty cache table. */
53	    lua_newtable(L);
54	    lua_setfield(L, LUA_REGISTRYINDEX, LUA_CACHE_KEY);
55	    return L;
56	}
57	/* }}} */
58	
59	/* {{{ lua_teardown() */
60	static void lua_teardown(void *handle)
61	{
62	    if (handle) lua_close((lua_State *)handle);
63	}
64	/* }}} */
65	
66	/* {{{ lua_invoke() */
67	static int lua_invoke(void *handle,
68	                      const char  *file_path,
69	                      const char  *fn_name,
70	                      const void **input_data,
71	                      const int   *input_sizes,
72	                      int          n_inputs,
73	                      void        *out_buf,
74	                      int          out_buf_capacity,
75	                      int         *out_size)
76	{
77	    lua_State *L = (lua_State *)handle;
78	    if (!L || !file_path || !fn_name) return -1;
79	
80	    int baseline = lua_gettop(L);
81	
82	    /* Module cache lookup. The cache table lives in the registry
83	     * under LUA_CACHE_KEY; each key is a file path, each value is
84	     * the module table the file's chunk returned. Stack after this
85	     * block: ..., cache, module. */
86	    lua_getfield(L, LUA_REGISTRYINDEX, LUA_CACHE_KEY);
87	    lua_getfield(L, -1, file_path);
88	    if (lua_isnil(L, -1)) {
89	        lua_pop(L, 1);   /* discard nil */
90	        /* Stack: ..., cache */
91	
92	        if (luaL_loadfile(L, file_path) != 0) {
93	            fprintf(stderr, "lua spec: loadfile('%s'): %s\n",
94	                    file_path, lua_tostring(L, -1));
95	            lua_settop(L, baseline);
96	            return -1;
97	        }
98	        if (lua_pcall(L, 0, 1, 0) != 0) {
99	            fprintf(stderr, "lua spec: chunk exec '%s': %s\n",
100	                    file_path, lua_tostring(L, -1));
101	            lua_settop(L, baseline);
102	            return -1;
103	        }
104	        if (!lua_istable(L, -1)) {
105	            fprintf(stderr, "lua spec: '%s' did not return a table\n", file_path);
106	            lua_settop(L, baseline);
107	            return -1;
108	        }
109	        /* Stack: ..., cache, module. Cache the module under
110	         * file_path. */
111	        lua_pushvalue(L, -1);
112	        lua_setfield(L, -3, file_path);
113	    }
114	    /* Stack at this point: ..., cache, module. */
115	
116	    /* Pull the function out of the module table. */
117	    lua_getfield(L, -1, fn_name);
118	    if (!lua_isfunction(L, -1)) {
119	        fprintf(stderr, "lua spec: '%s' is not a function in %s\n",
120	                fn_name, file_path);
121	        lua_settop(L, baseline);
122	        return -1;
123	    }
124	    /* Stack: ..., cache, module, function. Drop module + cache. */
125	    lua_remove(L, -2);   /* drop module */
126	    lua_remove(L, -2);   /* drop cache  */
127	
128	    /* Push each input as a Lua string. */
129	    for (int i = 0; i < n_inputs; i++) {
130	        if (input_data[i] == NULL && input_sizes[i] > 0) {
131	            fprintf(stderr, "lua spec: input %d is NULL with size %d\n",
132	                    i, input_sizes[i]);
133	            lua_settop(L, baseline);
134	            return -1;
135	        }
136	        lua_pushlstring(L, (const char *)input_data[i], (size_t)input_sizes[i]);
137	    }
138	
139	    /* Call. One return value expected. */
140	    if (lua_pcall(L, n_inputs, 1, 0) != 0) {
141	        fprintf(stderr, "lua spec: %s.%s: %s\n",
142	                file_path, fn_name, lua_tostring(L, -1));
143	        lua_settop(L, baseline);
144	        return -1;
145	    }
146	
147	    /* Convert the return to a string. tostring handles numbers /
148	     * booleans / nil; if the spec needs richer marshaling later
149	     * (tables, structs), that's an issue-312-shaped change. */
150	    size_t len = 0;
151	    const char *result = lua_tolstring(L, -1, &len);
152	    if (!result) {
153	        /* Non-coercible value. For now, surface as zero-length
154	         * output rather than an error — the box returned something
155	         * we can't serialize, but the call itself succeeded. */
156	        if (out_size) *out_size = 0;
157	        lua_settop(L, baseline);
158	        return 0;
159	    }
160	    if ((int)len > out_buf_capacity) {
161	        fprintf(stderr, "lua spec: %s.%s returned %zu bytes; "
162	                        "buffer is %d\n",
163	                file_path, fn_name, len, out_buf_capacity);
164	        lua_settop(L, baseline);
165	        return -1;
166	    }
167	    if (out_buf && len > 0) memcpy(out_buf, result, len);
168	    if (out_size) *out_size = (int)len;
169	
170	    lua_settop(L, baseline);
171	    return 0;
172	}
173	/* }}} */
174	
175	/* {{{ soramech_lang_spec */
176	lang_spec_t soramech_lang_spec = {
177	    .name     = "lua",
178	    .file_ext = ".lua",
179	    .init     = lua_init,
180	    .teardown = lua_teardown,
181	    .compile  = 0,
182	    .invoke   = lua_invoke,
183	};
184	/* }}} */
185

1	#!/bin/bash
2	# scripts/run-tests.sh — phase 3 integration tests.
3	#
4	# Runs every fixture map under tests/maps/ through `soramech-pool`
5	# and asserts that the captured outputs match a hardcoded
6	# expectation per map. The point is to catch regressions
7	# end-to-end — past the spec interface, past the dispatch action,
8	# past JSONL writing — rather than via the in-process unit tests.
9	#
10	# Per the project convention: scripts run from any directory via a
11	# hard-coded ${DIR} path with an override argument; all paths are
12	# relative to ${DIR}.
13	
14	set -u
15	
16	DIR="/mnt/mtwo/programs/sora/soramech"
17	if [[ $# -ge 1 ]]; then
18	    DIR="$1"
19	fi
20	
21	cd "$DIR"
22	
23	pass=0
24	fail=0
25	failures=()
26	
27	# {{{ check_map() — run one fixture, verify expected substrings
28	check_map() {
29	    local map="$1"
30	    shift
31	    local expected=("$@")
32	
33	    printf "  %-44s " "$map"
34	
35	    local output
36	    output=$("$DIR/soramech-pool" "$DIR/tests/maps/$map" 2>&1)
37	    local rc=$?
38	
39	    local missing=""
40	    if [[ $rc -ne 0 ]]; then
41	        missing="exit=$rc"
42	    fi
43	    for e in "${expected[@]}"; do
44	        if [[ "$output" != *"$e"* ]]; then
45	            missing+="${missing:+; }missing '$e'"
46	        fi
47	    done
48	
49	    if [[ -z "$missing" ]]; then
50	        printf "ok\n"
51	        pass=$((pass + 1))
52	    else
53	        printf "FAIL — %s\n" "$missing"
54	        fail=$((fail + 1))
55	        failures+=("$map: $missing")
56	    fi
57	}
58	# }}}
59

72	        else
73	            printf "FAIL — expected '11!', got '%s'\n" "$content"
74	            fail=$((fail + 1))
75	            failures+=("pipeline file: $content")
76	        fi
77	    else
78	        printf "FAIL — output file missing\n"
79	        fail=$((fail + 1))
80	        failures+=("pipeline: $file missing")
81	    fi
82	}
83	# }}}

86	# Verifies issue 309's compile pipeline: bundles all spec.so + sources
87	# into compiled/, then runs the artifact from /tmp (i.e. with no

# langs/lua/Makefile — build the Lua language spec.
#
# Produces spec.so, a small .so the pool runner dlopens at startup.
# Links against LuaJIT 2.1 (override LUA_INCLUDE / LUA_LIB if the
# headers and library live somewhere other than the Debian/Arch
# default).
#
# Invokable standalone (`make -C langs/lua`) or from the top-level
# Makefile via `make specs`. The DIR variable lets the top-level
# pass the project root so the -I path to langs/lang-spec.h resolves.

# {{{ Resolve project root
DIR ?= $(abspath $(dir $(lastword $(MAKEFILE_LIST)))/../..)
# }}}

# {{{ Toolchain & flags
CC          ?= gcc
LUA_INCLUDE ?= /usr/include/luajit-2.1
LUA_LIB     ?= /usr/lib

CFLAGS  = -shared -fPIC -O2 -Wall -I$(LUA_INCLUDE) -I$(DIR)/langs
LDFLAGS = -L$(LUA_LIB) -lluajit-5.1
# }}}

# {{{ Build rules
.PHONY: all clean

all: spec.so

spec.so: spec.c $(DIR)/langs/lang-spec.h
	@$(CC) $(CFLAGS) -o $@ $< $(LDFLAGS)

clean:
	@rm -f spec.so
# }}}
```

1	/* libs/json/json.h — small DOM JSON parser + streaming writer.
2	 *
3	 * Written for SoraMech, not vendored. Designed in issue 314. Two
4	 * halves: a DOM parser with arena-allocated nodes (used by the
5	 * phase 3 graph loader, issue 305), and a streaming writer with a
6	 * fixed buffer (used by the JSONL run-log writer, issue 311).
7	 *
8	 * The parser is hand-rolled recursive descent. The DOM is read-only
9	 * once parsed; constructing JSON goes through the writer, not the
10	 * DOM. Numbers are doubles; integer-shaped fields read via
11	 * (int)json_number_value(...). UTF-8 strict, BMP \uXXXX with
12	 * surrogate-pair handling. No comments, no trailing commas.
13	 *
14	 * Errors: json_parse returns NULL on failure, with *err_offset
15	 * and *err_msg filled. The graph loader maps the offset to a
16	 * filename:line:column diagnostic before surfacing it.
17	 *
18	 * Memory: every parse tree lives in one json_arena_t. Destroying
19	 * the arena frees every node and every string in one call. There
20	 * is no per-node free.
21	 *
22	 * Threading: single-threaded — the parser is meant to run on the
23	 * graph-load thread before the pool spins up. Concurrency comes in
24	 * at the writer side, where each writer call is independent and the
25	 * caller owns the buffer.
26	 */

29	#define SORAMECH_JSON_H

31	#include <stddef.h>

33	#ifdef __cplusplus

39	    JSON_NULL,
40	    JSON_BOOL,
41	    JSON_NUMBER,
42	    JSON_STRING,
43	    JSON_ARRAY,
44	    JSON_OBJECT,
45	} json_kind_t;

50	
51	/* {{{ Arena lifecycle */
52	/* Construct an empty arena. Returns NULL on allocation failure. */
53	json_arena_t *json_arena_create(void);
54	
55	/* Free every chunk in the arena. Safe on NULL. */
56	void          json_arena_destroy(json_arena_t *a);
57	
58	/* Bytes currently allocated across all chunks (for diagnostics). */
59	size_t        json_arena_bytes(const json_arena_t *a);
60	/* }}} */
61	
62	/* {{{ Parser */
63	/* Parse a NUL-terminated UTF-8 source into a tree owned by the
64	 * arena. Returns NULL on syntax error; *err_offset (if non-NULL)
65	 * receives the byte offset where parsing failed, and *err_msg
66	 * (if non-NULL) receives a static-string description. */
67	json_node_t *json_parse(json_arena_t *a,
68	                        const char  *src,
69	                        int         *err_offset,
70	                        const char **err_msg);
71	
72	/* Read a file end-to-end and parse it. *err_line (if non-NULL) is
73	 * the 1-based line number where parsing failed, computed from the
74	 * byte offset against the file contents. */
75	json_node_t *json_parse_file(json_arena_t *a,
76	                             const char  *path,
77	                             int         *err_line,
78	                             const char **err_msg);
79	/* }}} */
80	
81	/* {{{ Accessors */
82	json_kind_t   json_kind        (const json_node_t *n);
83	int           json_is_null     (const json_node_t *n);
84	int           json_bool_value  (const json_node_t *n);
85	double        json_number_value(const json_node_t *n);
86	
87	/* NUL-terminated; lives in the arena. */
88	const char   *json_string_value(const json_node_t *n);
89	
90	int           json_array_size  (const json_node_t *n);
91	json_node_t  *json_array_at    (const json_node_t *n, int i);
92	
93	int           json_object_size (const json_node_t *n);
94	const char   *json_object_key  (const json_node_t *n, int i);
95	json_node_t  *json_object_value(const json_node_t *n, int i);
96	
97	/* Linear scan; returns NULL if key not present. */

99	/* }}} */

106	    int   err;        /* 0 ok; nonzero = truncation or bad call */
107	    /* Internal: depth stack (commas + bracket pairing). */
108	    unsigned char depth;

110	} json_writer_t;

### Tool result

```
# SoraMech — top-level build orchestration.
#
# What it does, in a sentence: builds the phase 3 pool runner binary
# plus one shared library per language spec. Release by default;
# DEBUG=1 keeps symbols and disables optimization, STRICT=1 promotes
# warnings to errors. `make clean` removes everything that was built.
#
# Invokable from any directory: `make -C /path/to/soramech` works,
# and the DIR variable can be overridden if needed (e.g. for an
# out-of-tree checkout). All paths in this Makefile are relative to
# $(DIR) per the project convention.
#
# Designed in issue 309. Implementation of the runner's runtime is
# split across issues 301–308; this Makefile builds whatever sources
# exist under src/, libs/task-pool/, and libs/json/, so it scales as
# those issues land.

# {{{ Resolve project root
DIR ?= $(abspath $(dir $(lastword $(MAKEFILE_LIST))))
# }}}

# {{{ Toolchain & flags
CC      ?= gcc

# Shared header search path: every C source can find langs/lang-spec.h
# and any future libs/<name>/ headers via -I flags below.
CPPFLAGS = -I$(DIR)/langs -I$(DIR)/libs/task-pool -I$(DIR)/libs/json -I$(DIR)/src

# Default to release; DEBUG and STRICT toggle alternates.
ifeq ($(DEBUG),1)
  CFLAGS = -O0 -g -DDEBUG -Wall -pthread -fPIC
else ifeq ($(STRICT),1)
  CFLAGS = -O2 -Wall -Wextra -Wpedantic -Werror -pthread -fPIC
else
  CFLAGS = -O2 -Wall -pthread -fPIC
endif

LDFLAGS = -ldl -pthread
# }}}

# {{{ Paths and source discovery
BUILD_DIR = $(DIR)/build
POOL_BIN  = $(DIR)/soramech-pool

# Wildcards: empty subdirs return empty lists, which is fine. As
# issues 301/302/303/304/305 land their C sources here, they get
# compiled automatically — no Makefile edit required.
POOL_SOURCES = \
  $(wildcard $(DIR)/src/*.c) \
  $(wildcard $(DIR)/libs/task-pool/*.c) \
  $(wildcard $(DIR)/libs/json/*.c)

POOL_OBJECTS = $(POOL_SOURCES:$(DIR)/%.c=$(BUILD_DIR)/%.o)

# Test sources live in tests/ as C files named to match the source
# they exercise (e.g. tests/009-slot-store-test.c exercises
# src/009-slot-store.c). Each builds to a separate executable that
# `make test` runs in turn.
TEST_SOURCES = $(wildcard $(DIR)/tests/*.c)
TEST_BINS    = $(TEST_SOURCES:$(DIR)/tests/%.c=$(BUILD_DIR)/tests/%)

LANGS = lua c bash
# }}}

# {{{ Public targets
.PHONY: all runner specs clean test help

all: runner specs

runner: $(POOL_BIN)

specs:
	@for lang in $(LANGS); do \
	  echo "  → langs/$$lang"; \
	  $(MAKE) -s -C $(DIR)/langs/$$lang DIR=$(DIR) || exit 1; \
	done

clean:
	rm -f $(POOL_BIN)
	rm -rf $(BUILD_DIR)
```

## Assistant

Hey. Picking back up — we were about to start implementing the Lua spec's JSON bridges (`native_to_json` / `json_to_native`).

Before I touch code, one thing to confirm: the bridge signature in `langs/lang-spec.h:53` is

```c
typedef int (*lang_bridge_fn)(const void *src, int src_size,
                              void *dst, int dst_capacity, int *dst_size);
```

That's missing the worker handle (the `lua_State *` for the Lua spec). To encode/decode a Lua value, the bridge needs access to the state — either to read the value off the top of the stack (encode) or push the parsed value to the top (decode). The convention from issue 317 was: top-of-stack is the protocol.

I want to add `void *handle` as the first parameter. No callers exist yet (only the typedef + the NULL slots in each spec struct), so it's a safe change. Sound good, or did you want a different shape — e.g. pass the JSON bytes through stdin/stdout-ish buffers and let the spec keep its own state-juggling internal?

json.c
json.h
json.info.md
# SoraMech — top-level build orchestration.
#
# What it does, in a sentence: builds the phase 3 pool runner binary
# plus one shared library per language spec. Release by default;
# DEBUG=1 keeps symbols and disables optimization, STRICT=1 promotes
# warnings to errors. `make clean` removes everything that was built.
#
# Invokable from any directory: `make -C /path/to/soramech` works,
# and the DIR variable can be overridden if needed (e.g. for an
# out-of-tree checkout). All paths in this Makefile are relative to
# $(DIR) per the project convention.
#
# Designed in issue 309. Implementation of the runner's runtime is
# split across issues 301–308; this Makefile builds whatever sources
# exist under src/, libs/task-pool/, and libs/json/, so it scales as
# those issues land.

# {{{ Resolve project root
DIR ?= $(abspath $(dir $(lastword $(MAKEFILE_LIST))))
# }}}

# {{{ Toolchain & flags
CC      ?= gcc

# Shared header search path: every C source can find langs/lang-spec.h
# and any future libs/<name>/ headers via -I flags below.
CPPFLAGS = -I$(DIR)/langs -I$(DIR)/libs/task-pool -I$(DIR)/libs/json -I$(DIR)/src

# Default to release; DEBUG and STRICT toggle alternates.
ifeq ($(DEBUG),1)
  CFLAGS = -O0 -g -DDEBUG -Wall -pthread -fPIC
else ifeq ($(STRICT),1)
  CFLAGS = -O2 -Wall -Wextra -Wpedantic -Werror -pthread -fPIC
else
  CFLAGS = -O2 -Wall -pthread -fPIC
endif

LDFLAGS = -ldl -pthread
# }}}

# {{{ Paths and source discovery
BUILD_DIR = $(DIR)/build
POOL_BIN  = $(DIR)/soramech-pool

# Wildcards: empty subdirs return empty lists, which is fine. As
# issues 301/302/303/304/305 land their C sources here, they get
# compiled automatically — no Makefile edit required.
POOL_SOURCES = \
  $(wildcard $(DIR)/src/*.c) \
  $(wildcard $(DIR)/libs/task-pool/*.c) \
  $(wildcard $(DIR)/libs/json/*.c)

POOL_OBJECTS = $(POOL_SOURCES:$(DIR)/%.c=$(BUILD_DIR)/%.o)

# Test sources live in tests/ as C files named to match the source
# they exercise (e.g. tests/009-slot-store-test.c exercises
# src/009-slot-store.c). Each builds to a separate executable that
# `make test` runs in turn.
TEST_SOURCES = $(wildcard $(DIR)/tests/*.c)
TEST_BINS    = $(TEST_SOURCES:$(DIR)/tests/%.c=$(BUILD_DIR)/tests/%)

LANGS = lua c bash
# }}}

# {{{ Public targets
.PHONY: all runner specs clean test help

all: runner specs

runner: $(POOL_BIN)

specs:
	@for lang in $(LANGS); do \
	  echo "  → langs/$$lang"; \
	  $(MAKE) -s -C $(DIR)/langs/$$lang DIR=$(DIR) || exit 1; \
	done

clean:
	rm -f $(POOL_BIN)
	rm -rf $(BUILD_DIR)
```
